Binary search: target 33 in a ten-element array JAH-PSCAS-000001 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a programming problem: binary search: target 33 in a ten-element array. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Binary search is the fastest way to find a value in a sorted array: each probe discards half of what is left, so ten elements need at most four probes. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

History

Binary search is one of the oldest algorithms in computing; it appears in the earliest sorting and searching literature and in every standard algorithms textbook, including Knuth's The Art of Computer Programming. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: binary search: target 33 in a ten-element array. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Binary search is the fastest way to find a value in a sorted array: each probe discards half of what is left, so ten elements need at most four probes. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Search for the value 33 in the sorted array [3, 7, 11, 15, 19, 27, 33, 41, 55, 68] using binary search. Give the index (0-based), the probe trace, and the number of comparisons.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Confirm the precondition: the array is sorted in increasing order: 3, 7, 11, 15, 19, 27, 33, 41, 55, 68.
  2. Step 2: Set lo = 0 and hi = 9, the first and last indices.
  3. Step 3: Probe the middle: mid = (lo + hi) // 2. If the middle element equals the target, stop; if it is smaller, set lo = mid + 1; if larger, set hi = mid - 1.
  4. Step 4: Repeat on the remaining half. The demo's probe trace is [(0, 9, 4), (5, 9, 7), (5, 6, 5), (6, 6, 6)].
  5. Step 5: The target is found at index 6 after 4 comparisons.
  6. Step 6: Check: the element at index 6 is the target, and the search halved the range at every step, which is why it took only 4 comparisons for ten elements.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The target 33 is at index 6 (0-based) in the sorted array, found after 4 comparisons. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure searches any sorted array of any comparable type, from numbers to strings, given the same ordering. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if the array is sorted. On an unsorted array binary search can miss a present value or report a wrong index; the algorithm also assumes random access to elements by index. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each probe halves the search range, so the number of probes grows with the logarithm of the array size: O(log n) comparisons, with O(1) extra space. The demo counts the comparisons actually performed on the stated input, and the count is consistent with the logarithmic bound. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because the sorted order guarantees the target, if present, lies in the half that is kept: the middle element splits the array, and the comparison tells which side can contain the target. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [3, 7, 11, 15, 19, 27, 33, 41, 55, 68], 'target': 33}
def solve(P):
    arr = list(P['arr'])
    target = P['target']
    lo, hi = 0, len(arr) - 1
    comparisons = 0
    trace = []
    index = -1
    while lo <= hi:
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        comparisons += 1
        trace.append((lo, hi, mid))
        if arr[mid] == target:
            index = mid
            break
        elif arr[mid] < target:
            lo = mid + 1
        else:
            hi = mid - 1
    return {'index': index, 'comparisons': comparisons, 'trace': trace, 'found': index >= 0, 'arr_str': ', '.join(map(str, arr))}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Array:', P['arr'])
print('Target:', P['target'])
print('Probes (lo, hi, mid):', R['trace'])
print('Index:', R['index'])
print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Target: 33
Probes (lo, hi, mid): [(0, 9, 4), (5, 9, 7), (5, 6, 5), (6, 6, 6)]
Index: 6
Comparisons: 4
Run 1 SHA-256: 7cc4162926b022351a5724b70e3b2dd0bc01fb2c0e244fc9d6ec92821962dca5
Run 2 SHA-256: 7cc4162926b022351a5724b70e3b2dd0bc01fb2c0e244fc9d6ec92821962dca5
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Binary search: target 33 in a ten-element array """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [3, 7, 11, 15, 19, 27, 33, 41, 55, 68], 'target': 33}
    def solve(self, P):
        arr = list(P['arr'])
        target = P['target']
        lo, hi = 0, len(arr) - 1
        comparisons = 0
        trace = []
        index = -1
        while lo <= hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            comparisons += 1
            trace.append((lo, hi, mid))
            if arr[mid] == target:
                index = mid
                break
            elif arr[mid] < target:
                lo = mid + 1
            else:
                hi = mid - 1
        return {'index': index, 'comparisons': comparisons, 'trace': trace, 'found': index >= 0, 'arr_str': ', '.join(map(str, arr))}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Array:', P['arr'])
        print('Target:', P['target'])
        print('Probes (lo, hi, mid):', R['trace'])
        print('Index:', R['index'])
        print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Binary search: target 33 in a ten-element array """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [3, 7, 11, 15, 19, 27, 33, 41, 55, 68], 'target': 33}

        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Bubble sort: nine unsorted numbers JAH-PSCAS-000002 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a programming problem: bubble sort: nine unsorted numbers. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Bubble sort is the simplest sorting algorithm to trace: neighbours are compared and swapped until the largest elements have bubbled to the end, one pass at a time. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

History

Bubble sort is among the earliest sorting methods described in computing; its name comes from the way larger elements rise to the top like bubbles, and it remains the standard first sorting algorithm taught to beginners. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: bubble sort: nine unsorted numbers. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Bubble sort is the simplest sorting algorithm to trace: neighbours are compared and swapped until the largest elements have bubbled to the end, one pass at a time. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Sort the list [5, 2, 9, 1, 7, 6, 3, 8, 4] with bubble sort. Give the sorted list, the number of passes, and the number of swaps.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start with the unsorted list of 9 elements.
  2. Step 2: Pass 1: walk along the list comparing neighbours and swapping each pair that is out of order, so the largest element bubbles to the end.
  3. Step 3: Repeat: each pass places the next largest element, and the sorted tail grows by one.
  4. Step 4: Stop early when a full pass makes no swaps, which means the list is sorted.
  5. Step 5: The result is 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, reached in 6 passes with 17 swaps.
  6. Step 6: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input, which the demo verifies by printing both.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The sorted list is 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, reached in 6 passes with 17 swaps. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure sorts any list of comparable elements, given the same comparison. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for small lists and for teaching; for large lists bubble sort is far too slow, and a faster sort (merge sort, quick sort, or the language's built-in sort) should be used. It is a stable sort, but its quadratic worst case is the real limit. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each pass makes up to n comparisons, and up to n passes are needed, so the worst case is O(n^2) comparisons and swaps, with O(1) extra space. The demo counts the passes and swaps actually performed on the stated input. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because each pass moves the largest remaining element to its final position at the end of the unsorted part, so after enough passes every element is in its final position. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [5, 2, 9, 1, 7, 6, 3, 8, 4]}
def solve(P):
    a = list(P['arr'])
    n = len(a)
    swaps = 0
    passes = 0
    for i in range(n):
        swapped = False
        passes += 1
        for j in range(0, n - i - 1):
            if a[j] > a[j + 1]:
                a[j], a[j + 1] = a[j + 1], a[j]
                swaps += 1
                swapped = True
        if not swapped:
            break
    return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'passes': passes, 'swaps': swaps, 'n': n}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Passes:', R['passes'])
print('Swaps:', R['swaps'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Bubble sort: nine unsorted numbers """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [5, 2, 9, 1, 7, 6, 3, 8, 4]}
    def solve(self, P):
        a = list(P['arr'])
        n = len(a)
        swaps = 0
        passes = 0
        for i in range(n):
            swapped = False
            passes += 1
            for j in range(0, n - i - 1):
                if a[j] > a[j + 1]:
                    a[j], a[j + 1] = a[j + 1], a[j]
                    swaps += 1
                    swapped = True
            if not swapped:
                break
        return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'passes': passes, 'swaps': swaps, 'n': n}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Input:', P['arr'])
        print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
        print('Passes:', R['passes'])
        print('Swaps:', R['swaps'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

History

Bubble sort is among the earliest sorting methods described in computing; its name comes from the way larger elements rise to the top like bubbles, and it remains the standard first sorting algorithm taught to beginners. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Problem

Sort the list [5, 2, 9, 1, 7, 6, 3, 8, 4] with bubble sort. Give the sorted list, the number of passes, and the number of swaps.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Start with the unsorted list of 9 elements. Step 2: Pass 1: walk along the list comparing neighbours and swapping each pair that is out of order, so the largest element bubbles to the end. Step 3: Repeat: each pass places the next largest element, and the sorted tail grows by one. Step 4: Stop early when a full pass makes no swaps, which means the list is sorted. Step 5: The result is 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, reached in 6 passes with 17 swaps. Step 6: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input, which the demo verifies by printing both. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Input list stated in the problem; algorithm from standard textbooks.

Formal equation

T(n) = O(n^2)

Formal equation, in plain words

T(n) is the number of comparisons for n elements; bubble sort makes up to n passes of up to n comparisons.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

passes x comparisons per pass = O(n^2)

Proof sketch

The solution is checked by execution. First, the demo runs the algorithm on the stated input: it runs bubble sort on the stated list and prints the sorted list, the pass count, and the swap count. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the edge cases named in the worked solution are also executed and their results printed. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

Results reported

The sorted list is 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, reached in 6 passes with 17 swaps. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds for small lists and for teaching; for large lists bubble sort is far too slow, and a faster sort (merge sort, quick sort, or the language's built-in sort) should be used. It is a stable sort, but its quadratic worst case is the real limit. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

The Problem

Sort the list [41, 8, 23, 17, 5, 30] with selection sort. Give the sorted list, the number of passes, and the number of swaps.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start with the unsorted list of 6 elements.
  2. Step 2: Pass 1: scan the whole list for the smallest element and swap it into position 0.
  3. Step 3: Repeat: each pass finds the smallest of the remaining elements and swaps it into the next position.
  4. Step 4: After 5 passes the list is sorted: 5, 8, 17, 23, 30, 41, with 3 swaps.
  5. Step 5: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The sorted list is 5, 8, 17, 23, 30, 41, reached in 5 passes with 3 swaps. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure sorts any list of comparable elements, given the same comparison. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for small lists; for large lists the quadratic comparison count is too slow, and selection sort is not stable, so equal elements can change their relative order. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each pass scans the remaining elements, so the total comparisons are n(n-1)/2, which is O(n^2), while the swaps are at most n-1, with O(1) extra space. The demo counts the passes and swaps actually performed. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because after each pass the first k elements are the k smallest, in order: the pass selects the minimum of the rest, so the sorted prefix only grows. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [41, 8, 23, 17, 5, 30]}
def solve(P):
    a = list(P['arr'])
    n = len(a)
    swaps = 0
    for i in range(n - 1):
        min_i = i
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if a[j] < a[min_i]:
                min_i = j
        if min_i != i:
            a[i], a[min_i] = a[min_i], a[i]
            swaps += 1
    return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'passes': n - 1, 'swaps': swaps, 'n': n}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Passes:', R['passes'])
print('Swaps:', R['swaps'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Selection sort: six unsorted numbers """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [41, 8, 23, 17, 5, 30]}
    def solve(self, P):
        a = list(P['arr'])
        n = len(a)
        swaps = 0
        for i in range(n - 1):
            min_i = i
            for j in range(i + 1, n):
                if a[j] < a[min_i]:
                    min_i = j
            if min_i != i:
                a[i], a[min_i] = a[min_i], a[i]
                swaps += 1
        return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'passes': n - 1, 'swaps': swaps, 'n': n}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Input:', P['arr'])
        print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
        print('Passes:', R['passes'])
        print('Swaps:', R['swaps'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()

Specification

  • care: Do not rely on selection sort to preserve the order of equal elements; for large lists use a faster sort.
  • dimensions: {'arr': '41, 8, 23, 17, 5, 30', 'computed n': '6', 'computed passes': '5', 'computed swaps': '3'}

Boundless scales

  • A1: Scale A1 — larger inputs: the same selection sort with larger quantities, worked the same way and verified by the same demo.
  • A2: Scale A2 — a different instance: the same selection sort with different numbers and values (A longer list, a list with duplicate values, and a chained sort-to-median variant.), worked the same way.
  • A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the selection sort chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Key figures

  • arr: [41, 8, 23, 17, 5, 30]
  • n: 6
  • passes: 5
  • sorted: [5, 8, 17, 23, 30, 41]
  • swaps: 3

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [41, 8, 23, 17, 5, 30]}
def solve(P):
    a = list(P['arr'])
    n = len(a)
    swaps = 0
    for i in range(n - 1):
        min_i = i
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if a[j] < a[min_i]:
                min_i = j
        if min_i != i:
            a[i], a[min_i] = a[min_i], a[i]
            swaps += 1
    return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'passes': n - 1, 'swaps': swaps, 'n': n}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Passes:', R['passes'])
print('Swaps:', R['swaps'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

    """Presolved solver: Selection sort: six unsorted numbers """
    def __init__(self):

    def solve(self, P):
        a = list(P['arr'])
        n = len(a)
        swaps = 0

                swaps += 1

        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)

        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)

This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Sort the list [9, 4, 7, 2, 8, 1, 5] with insertion sort. Give the sorted list, the number of elements inserted, and the number of shifts.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start with the unsorted list of 7 elements; the first element alone is already a sorted list of one.
  2. Step 2: Take the next element and insert it into the sorted part by shifting larger elements one place to the right until its place is found.
  3. Step 3: Repeat for each remaining element until the whole list is sorted.
  4. Step 4: The result is 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, reached with 14 shifts.
  5. Step 5: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The sorted list is 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, reached with 14 shifts over 7 elements. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure sorts any list of comparable elements, given the same comparison. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for small or nearly sorted lists; for large random lists the quadratic worst case is too slow, and a faster sort should be used. It is stable, so equal elements keep their relative order. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each insertion shifts up to i elements, so the worst case is O(n^2) shifts, with O(1) extra space; on nearly sorted input it is near O(n). The demo counts the shifts actually performed on the stated input. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because the sorted prefix stays sorted after every insertion: the new element is placed exactly where it belongs among the sorted elements, so the prefix only grows and never breaks order. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [9, 4, 7, 2, 8, 1, 5]}
def solve(P):
    a = list(P['arr'])
    n = len(a)
    shifts = 0
    for i in range(1, n):
        key = a[i]
        j = i - 1
        while j >= 0 and a[j] > key:
            a[j + 1] = a[j]
            shifts += 1
            j -= 1
        a[j + 1] = key
    return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'shifts': shifts, 'n': n}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Elements inserted:', R['n'] - 1)
print('Shifts:', R['shifts'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Insertion sort: seven unsorted numbers """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [9, 4, 7, 2, 8, 1, 5]}
    def solve(self, P):
        a = list(P['arr'])
        n = len(a)
        shifts = 0
        for i in range(1, n):
            key = a[i]
            j = i - 1
            while j >= 0 and a[j] > key:
                a[j + 1] = a[j]
                shifts += 1
                j -= 1
            a[j + 1] = key
        return {'sorted': a, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, a)), 'shifts': shifts, 'n': n}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Input:', P['arr'])
        print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
        print('Elements inserted:', R['n'] - 1)
        print('Shifts:', R['shifts'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Merge sort: seven unsorted numbers JAH-PSCAS-000005 · compounded

Merge sort is the canonical divide-and-conquer sort: split the list in half, sort each half, and merge the sorted halves. History

Merge sort was described by John von Neumann in 1945 and is one of the first divide-and-conquer algorithms; it remains a standard sort and the basis of external sorting for data too large for memory. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: merge sort: seven unsorted numbers. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Merge sort is the canonical divide-and-conquer sort: split the list in half, sort each half, and merge the sorted halves. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Sort the list [50, 23, 9, 18, 61, 32, 7] with merge sort. Give the sorted list and the number of comparisons made while merging.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Split the list of 7 elements in half, and split each half again, until the pieces have one element each (a one-element list is already sorted).
  2. Step 2: Merge the pieces back together in order: at each merge, compare the heads of the two sorted halves and take the smaller, counting one comparison per step.
  3. Step 3: The merges combine 1-element pieces into 2-element pieces, then into 4-element pieces, until the whole list is one sorted list.
  4. Step 4: The result is 7, 9, 18, 23, 32, 50, 61, reached with 14 merge comparisons.
  5. Step 5: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The sorted list is 7, 9, 18, 23, 32, 50, 61, reached with 14 merge comparisons. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure sorts any list of comparable elements, given the same comparison. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for lists that fit in memory with room for the merge buffers; merge sort needs O(n) extra space, and for very large data the external variant is needed. It is stable, so equal elements keep their relative order. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. Math Proof

Splitting is O(log n) levels deep and each level merges n elements, so the total is O(n log n) comparisons, with O(n) extra space. The demo counts the merge comparisons actually performed on the stated input. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because merging two sorted lists always produces a sorted list (each step takes the smaller head), and the recursion sorts each half before it is merged. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [50, 23, 9, 18, 61, 32, 7]}
def solve(P):
    comp = [0]
    def ms(a):
        if len(a) <= 1:
            return a
        mid = len(a) // 2
        left = ms(a[:mid])
        right = ms(a[mid:])
        out = []
        i = j = 0
        while i < len(left) and j < len(right):
            comp[0] += 1
            if left[i] <= right[j]:
                out.append(left[i]); i += 1
            else:
                out.append(right[j]); j += 1
        out += left[i:]
        out += right[j:]
        return out
    s = ms(list(P['arr']))
    return {'sorted': s, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, s)), 'comparisons': comp[0], 'n': len(P['arr'])}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Merge comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Merge sort: seven unsorted numbers """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [50, 23, 9, 18, 61, 32, 7]}
    def solve(self, P):
        comp = [0]
        def ms(a):
            if len(a) <= 1:
                return a
            mid = len(a) // 2
            left = ms(a[:mid])
            right = ms(a[mid:])
            out = []
            i = j = 0
            while i < len(left) and j < len(right):
                comp[0] += 1
                if left[i] <= right[j]:
                    out.append(left[i]); i += 1
                else:
                    out.append(right[j]); j += 1
            out += left[i:]
            out += right[j:]
            return out
        s = ms(list(P['arr']))
        return {'sorted': s, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, s)), 'comparisons': comp[0], 'n': len(P['arr'])}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Input:', P['arr'])
        print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
        print('Merge comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

Merge sort was described by John von Neumann in 1945 and is one of the first divide-and-conquer algorithms; it remains a standard sort and the basis of external sorting for data too large for memory. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Problem

Sort the list [50, 23, 9, 18, 61, 32, 7] with merge sort. Give the sorted list and the number of comparisons made while merging.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Split the list of 7 elements in half, and split each half again, until the pieces have one element each (a one-element list is already sorted). Step 2: Merge the pieces back together in order: at each merge, compare the heads of the two sorted halves and take the smaller, counting one comparison per step. Step 3: The merges combine 1-element pieces into 2-element pieces, then into 4-element pieces, until the whole list is one sorted list. Step 4: The result is 7, 9, 18, 23, 32, 50, 61, reached with 14 merge comparisons. Step 5: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Input list stated in the problem; algorithm from standard textbooks.

The Problem

Sort the list [33, 12, 45, 8, 19, 27, 4] with quick sort (first element as pivot). Give the sorted list and the number of comparisons.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Pick the first element as the pivot and partition the rest into elements less than or equal to the pivot and elements greater than it.
  2. Step 2: Recurse on the two partitions, and combine: sorted less, then the pivot, then sorted greater.
  3. Step 3: The recursion ends on partitions of one element or none, which are already sorted.
  4. Step 4: The result is 4, 8, 12, 19, 27, 33, 45, reached with 12 comparisons.
  5. Step 5: Check: the output is in increasing order and contains exactly the same elements as the input.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The sorted list is 4, 8, 12, 19, 27, 33, 45, reached with 12 comparisons. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure sorts any list of comparable elements, given the same comparison. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for the stated input; with a first-element pivot an already sorted list is the worst case, and a better pivot (median of three, or random) is used in practice. Quick sort as written here is not stable. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

On average the partitions halve the list, giving O(n log n) comparisons; the worst case (an already sorted list with a first-element pivot) is O(n^2). The demo counts the comparisons actually performed on the stated input. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because partitioning places the pivot in its final position, with every smaller element before it and every larger element after it, and the recursion sorts each side independently. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [33, 12, 45, 8, 19, 27, 4]}
def solve(P):
    comp = [0]
    def qs(a):
        if len(a) <= 1:
            return a
        pivot = a[0]
        less = []
        greater = []
        for x in a[1:]:
            comp[0] += 1
            if x <= pivot:
                less.append(x)
            else:
                greater.append(x)
        return qs(less) + [pivot] + qs(greater)
    s = qs(list(P['arr']))
    return {'sorted': s, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, s)), 'comparisons': comp[0], 'n': len(P['arr'])}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Proof sketch

The solution is checked by execution. First, the demo runs the algorithm on the stated input: it runs quick sort on the stated list and prints the sorted list and the comparison count. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the edge cases named in the worked solution are also executed and their results printed. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Skeptical analysis

This holds for the stated input; with a first-element pivot an already sorted list is the worst case, and a better pivot (median of three, or random) is used in practice. Quick sort as written here is not stable. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Specification

  • care: Do not use a first-element pivot on sorted input in production; pick a random or median pivot instead.
  • dimensions: {'arr': '33, 12, 45, 8, 19, 27, 4', 'computed comparisons': '12', 'computed n': '7'}

Boundless scales

  • A1: Scale A1 — larger inputs: the same quick sort with larger quantities, worked the same way and verified by the same demo.
  • A2: Scale A2 — a different instance: the same quick sort with different numbers and values (A longer list, a median-of-three pivot, and a chained sort-to-search variant.), worked the same way.
  • A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the quick sort chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [33, 12, 45, 8, 19, 27, 4]}
def solve(P):
    comp = [0]
    def qs(a):
        if len(a) <= 1:
            return a
        pivot = a[0]
        less = []
        greater = []
        for x in a[1:]:
            comp[0] += 1
            if x <= pivot:
                less.append(x)
            else:
                greater.append(x)
        return qs(less) + [pivot] + qs(greater)
    s = qs(list(P['arr']))
    return {'sorted': s, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, s)), 'comparisons': comp[0], 'n': len(P['arr'])}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Input:', P['arr'])
print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Quick sort: seven unsorted numbers """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [33, 12, 45, 8, 19, 27, 4]}
    def solve(self, P):
        comp = [0]
        def qs(a):
            if len(a) <= 1:
                return a
            pivot = a[0]
            less = []
            greater = []
            for x in a[1:]:
                comp[0] += 1
                if x <= pivot:
                    less.append(x)
                else:
                    greater.append(x)
            return qs(less) + [pivot] + qs(greater)
        s = qs(list(P['arr']))
        return {'sorted': s, 'sorted_str': ', '.join(map(str, s)), 'comparisons': comp[0], 'n': len(P['arr'])}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Input:', P['arr'])
        print('Sorted:', R['sorted'])
        print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

The Problem

Search for the value 44 in the list [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66] using linear search. Give the index (0-based) and the number of comparisons.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start at the first element and compare each element with the target in turn.
  2. Step 2: The demo compares 4 elements before finding the target.
  3. Step 3: Stop at the first match: the target is at index 3.
  4. Step 4: Check: the element at index 3 is the target, and no earlier element equals it.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The target 44 is at index 3 (0-based), found after 4 comparisons. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure searches any list of any type, given an equality test. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for any list, sorted or not, but it is slow on long lists: a sorted list should use binary search instead. It returns the first match if the value occurs more than once. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

In the worst case every element is compared, so the cost is O(n) comparisons, with O(1) extra space; the average is n/2. The demo counts the comparisons actually performed on the stated input. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because it examines every element until it finds the target or exhausts the list, so it cannot miss a present value and cannot report a wrong index. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'arr': [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66], 'target': 44}
def solve(P):
    arr = list(P['arr'])
    target = P['target']
    comparisons = 0
    index = -1
    for i, x in enumerate(arr):
        comparisons += 1
        if x == target:
            index = i
            break
    return {'index': index, 'comparisons': comparisons, 'found': index >= 0, 'arr_str': ', '.join(map(str, arr))}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('List:', P['arr'])
print('Target:', P['target'])
print('Index:', R['index'])
print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Linear search: target 44 in a six-element list """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'arr': [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66], 'target': 44}
    def solve(self, P):
        arr = list(P['arr'])
        target = P['target']
        comparisons = 0
        index = -1
        for i, x in enumerate(arr):
            comparisons += 1
            if x == target:
                index = i
                break
        return {'index': index, 'comparisons': comparisons, 'found': index >= 0, 'arr_str': ', '.join(map(str, arr))}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('List:', P['arr'])
        print('Target:', P['target'])
        print('Index:', R['index'])
        print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Reversing a string is the textbook first string operation: read the characters backwards, and the result is the mirror image. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. The Problem

Reverse the string 'signature'. Give the reversed string and show that reversing it again recovers the original.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Take the string of 9 characters: signature.
  2. Step 2: Build the reversed string by reading the characters from last to first: erutangis.
  3. Step 3: Reverse the result again to check: reversing erutangis gives signature.
  4. Step 4: The round trip recovers the original, which confirms the reversal is correct.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The reverse of signature is erutangis. Reversing it again gives signature, the original string. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure reverses any string or list, given the same indexing. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for plain text strings; reversing Unicode text with combining characters or grapheme clusters needs care, since a naive reversal can split a character from its combining marks. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Reversal touches each character once, so the cost is O(n) time and O(n) space for a new string. The demo verifies the result by reversing twice and checking the round trip. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because each character of the output is the character at the mirrored position of the input, so the output is exactly the input read backwards. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'s': 'signature'}
def solve(P):
    s = P['s']
    rev = s[::-1]
    back = rev[::-1]
    return {'rev': rev, 'back': back, 'roundtrip': back == s, 'n': len(s)}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Original:', P['s'])
print('Reversed:', R['rev'])
print('Reversed again:', R['back'])
print('Round trip recovers original:', R['roundtrip'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Reverse a string: signature """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'s': 'signature'}
    def solve(self, P):
        s = P['s']
        rev = s[::-1]
        back = rev[::-1]
        return {'rev': rev, 'back': back, 'roundtrip': back == s, 'n': len(s)}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Original:', P['s'])
        print('Reversed:', R['rev'])
        print('Reversed again:', R['back'])
        print('Round trip recovers original:', R['roundtrip'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

String reversal is a standard exercise in every programming course and a building block of text processing, from palindromes to in-place array reversal. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. Problem

Reverse the string 'signature'. Give the reversed string and show that reversing it again recovers the original.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Take the string of 9 characters: signature. Step 2: Build the reversed string by reading the characters from last to first: erutangis. Step 3: Reverse the result again to check: reversing erutangis gives signature. Step 4: The round trip recovers the original, which confirms the reversal is correct. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

reverse(reverse(s)) = s

Proof sketch

The solution is checked by execution. Results reported

The reverse of signature is erutangis. Reversing it again gives signature, the original string. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds for plain text strings; reversing Unicode text with combining characters or grapheme clusters needs care, since a naive reversal can split a character from its combining marks. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

    def __init__(self):

    def solve(self, P):

        rev = s[::-1]
        back = rev[::-1]

The Problem

Check whether 'level' is a palindrome, and whether 'world' is a palindrome. A palindrome reads the same forwards and backwards.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Take the first string, level, and reverse it: level.
  2. Step 2: Compare the reversed string with the original: they are identical, so level is a palindrome: True.
  3. Step 3: Take the second string, world, and reverse it: dlrow.
  4. Step 4: Compare: the reversed string differs from the original, so world is not a palindrome: False.
  5. Step 5: Check: the test is exactly the definition, a string equals its own reversal.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

level is a palindrome: True. world is a palindrome: False. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same test checks any string, given the same definition of equality. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for exact matching; real-world palindrome checks usually ignore case, spaces and punctuation, which needs a normalisation step first. An empty string and a one-character string are palindromes by this definition. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Reversal and comparison each touch every character once, so the cost is O(n) time and O(n) space. The demo checks both strings and prints both verdicts. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because it is the definition applied directly: a string is a palindrome exactly when it equals its reversal, and the demo computes the reversal and compares. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'s1': 'level', 's2': 'world'}
def solve(P):
    r1 = P['s1'] == P['s1'][::-1]
    r2 = P['s2'] == P['s2'][::-1]
    return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2, 'rev1': P['s1'][::-1], 'rev2': P['s2'][::-1]}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('String 1:', P['s1'])
print('Reversed 1:', R['rev1'])
print('Is palindrome 1:', R['r1'])
print('String 2:', P['s2'])
print('Reversed 2:', R['rev2'])
print('Is palindrome 2:', R['r2'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Palindrome check: level and world """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'s1': 'level', 's2': 'world'}
    def solve(self, P):
        r1 = P['s1'] == P['s1'][::-1]
        r2 = P['s2'] == P['s2'][::-1]
        return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2, 'rev1': P['s1'][::-1], 'rev2': P['s2'][::-1]}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('String 1:', P['s1'])
        print('Reversed 1:', R['rev1'])
        print('Is palindrome 1:', R['r1'])
        print('String 2:', P['s2'])
        print('Reversed 2:', R['rev2'])
        print('Is palindrome 2:', R['r2'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Palindrome check: level and world """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'s1': 'level', 's2': 'world'}
    def solve(self, P):
        r1 = P['s1'] == P['s1'][::-1]
        r2 = P['s2'] == P['s2'][::-1]
        return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2, 'rev1': P['s1'][::-1], 'rev2': P['s2'][::-1]}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('String 1:', P['s1'])
        print('Reversed 1:', R['rev1'])
        print('Is palindrome 1:', R['r1'])
        print('String 2:', P['s2'])
        print('Reversed 2:', R['rev2'])
        print('Is palindrome 2:', R['r2'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()
