Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: fizzbuzz: numbers 1 to 20. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. FizzBuzz is the classic loop-and-condition exercise: divisibility by 3 and 5 decides the word, and the order of the tests decides everything. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Run FizzBuzz for the numbers 1 to 20: print 'Fizz' for multiples of 3, 'Buzz' for multiples of 5, 'FizzBuzz' for multiples of both, and the number otherwise. Give the full output and the counts of each kind.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: For each number from 1 to 20, test divisibility by 3 and by 5.
  2. Step 2: If it is divisible by both (that is, by 15), output FizzBuzz; if only by 3, Fizz; if only by 5, Buzz; otherwise the number itself.
  3. Step 3: The output is: 1, 2, Fizz, 4, Buzz, Fizz, 7, 8, Fizz, Buzz, 11, Fizz, 13, 14, FizzBuzz, 16, 17, Fizz, 19, Buzz.
  4. Step 4: Counts: Fizz 5, Buzz 3, FizzBuzz 1, plain numbers 11.
  5. Step 5: Check: the counts add to 20, and the 15th entry is FizzBuzz, as 15 is divisible by both 3 and 5.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

FizzBuzz from 1 to 20 gives: 1, 2, Fizz, 4, Buzz, Fizz, 7, 8, Fizz, Buzz, 11, Fizz, 13, 14, FizzBuzz, 16, 17, Fizz, 19, Buzz. Counts are Fizz 5, Buzz 3, FizzBuzz 1, plain 11. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same structure produces any FizzBuzz-style labelling, given the divisors and the range. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for the stated range and divisors 3 and 5; other divisors or ranges need the same structure with different constants, and the both-case must always be tested first. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

One pass over n numbers with constant work each gives O(n) time and O(n) space for the output. The demo runs the loop and prints the full output and the counts. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because the divisibility tests are checked in the right order: the both-case first, then the single cases, so no number is mislabelled. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'n': 20}
def solve(P):
    n = P['n']
    out = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if i % 15 == 0:
            out.append('FizzBuzz')
        elif i % 3 == 0:
            out.append('Fizz')
        elif i % 5 == 0:
            out.append('Buzz')
        else:
            out.append(str(i))
    fizz = sum(1 for x in out if x == 'Fizz')
    buzz = sum(1 for x in out if x == 'Buzz')
    fb = sum(1 for x in out if x == 'FizzBuzz')
    plain = n - fizz - buzz - fb
    return {'out': out, 'out_str': ', '.join(out), 'fizz': fizz, 'buzz': buzz, 'fb': fb, 'plain': plain, 'n': n}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Output:', R['out'])
print('Fizz count:', R['fizz'])
print('Buzz count:', R['buzz'])
print('FizzBuzz count:', R['fb'])
print('Plain numbers:', R['plain'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: FizzBuzz: numbers 1 to 20 """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'n': 20}
    def solve(self, P):
        n = P['n']
        out = []
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if i % 15 == 0:
                out.append('FizzBuzz')
            elif i % 3 == 0:
                out.append('Fizz')
            elif i % 5 == 0:
                out.append('Buzz')
            else:
                out.append(str(i))
        fizz = sum(1 for x in out if x == 'Fizz')
        buzz = sum(1 for x in out if x == 'Buzz')
        fb = sum(1 for x in out if x == 'FizzBuzz')
        plain = n - fizz - buzz - fb
        return {'out': out, 'out_str': ', '.join(out), 'fizz': fizz, 'buzz': buzz, 'fb': fb, 'plain': plain, 'n': n}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Output:', R['out'])
        print('Fizz count:', R['fizz'])
        print('Buzz count:', R['buzz'])
        print('FizzBuzz count:', R['fb'])
        print('Plain numbers:', R['plain'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

FizzBuzz began as a children's counting game and became the standard programming interview exercise; its fame as a filter comes from the observation that many candidates cannot write it. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Problem

Run FizzBuzz for the numbers 1 to 20: print 'Fizz' for multiples of 3, 'Buzz' for multiples of 5, 'FizzBuzz' for multiples of both, and the number otherwise. Give the full output and the counts of each kind.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: For each number from 1 to 20, test divisibility by 3 and by 5. Step 2: If it is divisible by both (that is, by 15), output FizzBuzz; if only by 3, Fizz; if only by 5, Buzz; otherwise the number itself. Step 3: The output is: 1, 2, Fizz, 4, Buzz, Fizz, 7, 8, Fizz, Buzz, 11, Fizz, 13, 14, FizzBuzz, 16, 17, Fizz, 19, Buzz. Step 4: Counts: Fizz 5, Buzz 3, FizzBuzz 1, plain numbers 11. Step 5: Check: the counts add to 20, and the 15th entry is FizzBuzz, as 15 is divisible by both 3 and 5. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Range stated in the problem; divisors 3 and 5.

Skeptical analysis

This holds for the stated range and divisors 3 and 5; other divisors or ranges need the same structure with different constants, and the both-case must always be tested first. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: breadth-first search: a six-node graph from c. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Breadth-first search explores a graph level by level: a queue guarantees that nearer nodes are always visited before farther ones. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Run breadth-first search (BFS) from node C on the graph C: [A, D], A: [C, B], B: [A, E], D: [C, F], E: [B, F], F: [D, E]. Give the visit order and the number of nodes visited.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start with a queue holding only the start node C, and mark it visited.
  2. Step 2: Take the first node from the queue, record it, and add its unvisited neighbours to the back of the queue.
  3. Step 3: Repeat until the queue is empty. The visit order is C -> A -> D -> B -> F -> E.
  4. Step 4: 6 nodes are visited, which is every node reachable from C.
  5. Step 5: Check: every node appears exactly once, neighbours are visited level by level, and the order is the order of increasing distance from the start.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

BFS from C visits C -> A -> D -> B -> F -> E. 6 nodes are visited in total. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure traverses any graph from any start node, given its adjacency lists. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for the stated connected graph; nodes unreachable from the start are not visited, and on a weighted graph BFS does not give shortest paths (Dijkstra's algorithm does). Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each node and each edge is processed once, so the cost is O(V + E) time and O(V) space. The demo runs BFS on the stated graph and prints the visit order. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because the queue is first-in first-out: nodes enter the queue in order of distance from the start, so they are visited in that order, and the visited set guarantees each node is processed once. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'graph': {'C': ['A', 'D'], 'A': ['C', 'B'], 'B': ['A', 'E'], 'D': ['C', 'F'], 'E': ['B', 'F'], 'F': ['D', 'E']}, 'start': 'C'}
def solve(P):
    graph = P['graph']
    start = P['start']
    seen = {start}
    order = []
    queue = [start]
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0)
        order.append(node)
        for nb in graph[node]:
            if nb not in seen:
                seen.add(nb)
                queue.append(nb)
    return {'order': order, 'order_str': ' -> '.join(order), 'visited': len(order)}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Start:', P['start'])
print('Visit order:', R['order'])
print('Nodes visited:', R['visited'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Breadth-first search: a six-node graph from C """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'graph': {'C': ['A', 'D'], 'A': ['C', 'B'], 'B': ['A', 'E'], 'D': ['C', 'F'], 'E': ['B', 'F'], 'F': ['D', 'E']}, 'start': 'C'}
    def solve(self, P):
        graph = P['graph']
        start = P['start']
        seen = {start}
        order = []
        queue = [start]
        while queue:
            node = queue.pop(0)
            order.append(node)
            for nb in graph[node]:
                if nb not in seen:
                    seen.add(nb)
                    queue.append(nb)
        return {'order': order, 'order_str': ' -> '.join(order), 'visited': len(order)}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Start:', P['start'])
        print('Visit order:', R['order'])
        print('Nodes visited:', R['visited'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()

Formal equation

visit order = increasing distance

Formal equation, in plain words

The visit order lists nodes by their distance (number of edges) from the start node, nearest first.

Formal status

Scientific equation

Proof sketch

The solution is checked by execution. First, the demo runs the algorithm on the stated input: it runs breadth-first search from the stated start node and prints the visit order and the node count. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the edge cases named in the worked solution are also executed and their results printed. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

BFS from C visits C -> A -> D -> B -> F -> E. 6 nodes are visited in total. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds for the stated connected graph; nodes unreachable from the start are not visited, and on a weighted graph BFS does not give shortest paths (Dijkstra's algorithm does). Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Specification

  • care: Mark a node visited when it enters the queue, not when it leaves, or it can be queued twice.
  • dimensions: {'computed visited': '6', 'graph': 'see problem statement', 'start': 'C'}

Boundless scales

  • A1: Scale A1 — larger inputs: the same breadth-first search with larger quantities, worked the same way and verified by the same demo.
  • A2: Scale A2 — a different instance: the same breadth-first search with different numbers and values (A different start node, a disconnected graph, and a chained BFS-to-shortest-path variant.), worked the same way.
  • A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the breadth-first search chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'graph': {'C': ['A', 'D'], 'A': ['C', 'B'], 'B': ['A', 'E'], 'D': ['C', 'F'], 'E': ['B', 'F'], 'F': ['D', 'E']}, 'start': 'C'}
def solve(P):
    graph = P['graph']
    start = P['start']
    seen = {start}
    order = []
    queue = [start]
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0)
        order.append(node)
        for nb in graph[node]:
            if nb not in seen:
                seen.add(nb)
                queue.append(nb)
    return {'order': order, 'order_str': ' -> '.join(order), 'visited': len(order)}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Start:', P['start'])
print('Visit order:', R['order'])
print('Nodes visited:', R['visited'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Breadth-first search: a six-node graph from C """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'graph': {'C': ['A', 'D'], 'A': ['C', 'B'], 'B': ['A', 'E'], 'D': ['C', 'F'], 'E': ['B', 'F'], 'F': ['D', 'E']}, 'start': 'C'}
    def solve(self, P):
        graph = P['graph']
        start = P['start']
        seen = {start}
        order = []
        queue = [start]
        while queue:
            node = queue.pop(0)
            order.append(node)
            for nb in graph[node]:
                if nb not in seen:
                    seen.add(nb)
                    queue.append(nb)
        return {'order': order, 'order_str': ' -> '.join(order), 'visited': len(order)}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Start:', P['start'])
        print('Visit order:', R['order'])
        print('Nodes visited:', R['visited'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

Depth-first search: a six-node graph from C JAH-PSCAS-000021 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a programming problem: depth-first search: a six-node graph from c. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Depth-first search explores a graph by going deep before going wide: follow the first neighbour as far as it goes, then backtrack. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

History

Depth-first search is one of the two fundamental graph traversals, described in the earliest graph-theory literature; it underlies maze solving, topological sorting, and cycle detection. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: depth-first search: a six-node graph from c. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Depth-first search explores a graph by going deep before going wide: follow the first neighbour as far as it goes, then backtrack. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Run depth-first search (DFS) from node C on the graph C: [A, D], A: [C, B], B: [A, E], D: [C, F], E: [B, F], F: [D, E], visiting neighbours in listed order. Give the visit order and the number of nodes visited.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start at C and mark it visited.
  2. Step 2: Go as deep as possible along the first unvisited neighbour, then backtrack and try the next neighbour, visiting neighbours in listed order.
  3. Step 3: The visit order is C -> A -> B -> E -> F -> D.
  4. Step 4: 6 nodes are visited, which is every node reachable from C.
  5. Step 5: Check: every node appears exactly once, and the order follows a depth-first path with backtracking, unlike the level order of BFS.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

DFS from C visits C -> A -> B -> E -> F -> D. 6 nodes are visited in total. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure traverses any graph from any start node, given its adjacency lists. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for the stated connected graph; nodes unreachable from the start are not visited, and the order depends on the neighbour listing. Deep graphs can hit the recursion limit, in which case an explicit stack is used. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each node and each edge is processed once, so the cost is O(V + E) time and O(V) space, plus the recursion depth. The demo runs DFS on the stated graph and prints the visit order. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because the recursion visits each reachable node exactly once (the visited set prevents repeats) and explores each neighbour fully before moving on, so the whole reachable component is covered. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

import math

def solve(P):
    graph = P['graph']
    start = P['start']
    seen = set()

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Depth-first search: a six-node graph from C """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'graph': {'C': ['A', 'D'], 'A': ['C', 'B'], 'B': ['A', 'E'], 'D': ['C', 'F'], 'E': ['B', 'F'], 'F': ['D', 'E']}, 'start': 'C'}
    def solve(self, P):
        graph = P['graph']
        start = P['start']
        seen = set()
        order = []
        def dfs(node):
            seen.add(node)
            order.append(node)
            for nb in graph[node]:
                if nb not in seen:
                    dfs(nb)
        dfs(start)
        return {'order': order, 'order_str': ' -> '.join(order), 'visited': len(order)}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Start:', P['start'])
        print('Visit order:', R['order'])
        print('Nodes visited:', R['visited'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Dijkstra's shortest path: a five-node weighted graph JAH-PSCAS-000022 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a programming problem: dijkstra's shortest path: a five-node weighted graph. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Dijkstra's algorithm finds shortest paths in a weighted graph by always settling the nearest unsettled node first: once a node is settled, its distance can never improve. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

History

Dijkstra's algorithm was published by Edsger Dijkstra in 1959 and is one of the most used algorithms in computing, underlying network routing, mapping, and navigation. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

The Problem

Find the shortest path from A to D in the weighted graph A-B 3, A-C 7, B-C 2, B-D 6, C-D 1, C-E 4, D-E 5, using Dijkstra's algorithm. Give the shortest distance and the path.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Start with distance 0 at A and infinity everywhere else; no node is settled yet.
  2. Step 2: Repeatedly settle the unsettled node with the smallest distance, and relax its edges: any neighbour reached more cheaply gets a better distance and a predecessor.
  3. Step 3: When D is settled, its distance is final: 6.
  4. Step 4: Follow the predecessors back from D to A: the path is A -> B -> C -> D.
  5. Step 5: Check: the path edges add to 6, and no alternative route is shorter, since Dijkstra settles nodes in order of distance.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The shortest path from A to D is A -> B -> C -> D with distance 6. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure finds shortest paths in any graph with non-negative weights, from any start to any target. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for non-negative edge weights; a negative weight breaks the settling argument, and a negative cycle makes shortest paths undefined. It also assumes the graph is connected enough for the target to be reachable. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

With a simple scan for the minimum the cost is O(V^2); with a priority queue it is O((V + E) log V). The demo runs Dijkstra on the stated graph and prints all distances and the shortest path. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because edge weights are non-negative, so the unsettled node with the smallest distance cannot be reached more cheaply through any other unsettled node; settling nodes in that order fixes each distance permanently. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'edges': [['A', 'B', 3], ['A', 'C', 7], ['B', 'C', 2], ['B', 'D', 6], ['C', 'D', 1], ['C', 'E', 4], ['D', 'E', 5]], 'start': 'A', 'end': 'D'}
def solve(P):
    graph = {}
    for u, v, w in P['edges']:
        graph.setdefault(u, []).append((v, w))
        graph.setdefault(v, []).append((u, w))
    start, end = P['start'], P['end']
    import math as _m
    dist = {node: _m.inf for node in graph}
    dist[start] = 0
    prev = {}
    done = set()
    while len(done) < len(graph):
        node = min((n for n in graph if n not in done), key=lambda n: dist[n])
        done.add(node)
        for nb, w in graph[node]:
            if dist[node] + w < dist[nb]:
                dist[nb] = dist[node] + w
                prev[nb] = node
    path = [end]
    while path[-1] != start:
        path.append(prev[path[-1]])
    path.reverse()
    return {'distance': dist[end], 'path': path, 'path_str': ' -> '.join(path), 'dist': {k: v for k, v in dist.items()}}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Distances:', R['dist'])
print('Shortest path:', R['path'])
print('Shortest distance:', R['distance'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Shortest distance: 6

Run 2 SHA-256: a3ee36dd20060410aef363a6a21a67d724a9f9f17d3b94b77f853552a42e3a39

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Dijkstra's shortest path: a five-node weighted graph """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'edges': [['A', 'B', 3], ['A', 'C', 7], ['B', 'C', 2], ['B', 'D', 6], ['C', 'D', 1], ['C', 'E', 4], ['D', 'E', 5]], 'start': 'A', 'end': 'D'}
    def solve(self, P):
        graph = {}
        for u, v, w in P['edges']:
            graph.setdefault(u, []).append((v, w))
            graph.setdefault(v, []).append((u, w))
        start, end = P['start'], P['end']
        import math as _m
        dist = {node: _m.inf for node in graph}
        dist[start] = 0
        prev = {}
        done = set()
        while len(done) < len(graph):
            node = min((n for n in graph if n not in done), key=lambda n: dist[n])
            done.add(node)
            for nb, w in graph[node]:
                if dist[node] + w < dist[nb]:
                    dist[nb] = dist[node] + w
                    prev[nb] = node
        path = [end]
        while path[-1] != start:
            path.append(prev[path[-1]])
        path.reverse()
        return {'distance': dist[end], 'path': path, 'path_str': ' -> '.join(path), 'dist': {k: v for k, v in dist.items()}}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Distances:', R['dist'])
        print('Shortest path:', R['path'])
        print('Shortest distance:', R['distance'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. Problem

Find the shortest path from A to D in the weighted graph A-B 3, A-C 7, B-C 2, B-D 6, C-D 1, C-E 4, D-E 5, using Dijkstra's algorithm. Give the shortest distance and the path.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Start with distance 0 at A and infinity everywhere else; no node is settled yet. Step 2: Repeatedly settle the unsettled node with the smallest distance, and relax its edges: any neighbour reached more cheaply gets a better distance and a predecessor. Step 3: When D is settled, its distance is final: 6. Step 4: Follow the predecessors back from D to A: the path is A -> B -> C -> D. Step 5: Check: the path edges add to 6, and no alternative route is shorter, since Dijkstra settles nodes in order of distance. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Weighted graph, start and end nodes stated in the problem.

Formal equation

dist(v) = min over u of dist(u) + w(u, v)

Formal equation, in plain words

dist(v) is the shortest distance to node v; u ranges over its neighbours; w(u, v) is the weight of the edge from u to v.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

settle nearest first

This record pre-solves a programming problem: bracket matching: curly brackets with a missing close. Bracket matching is the textbook stack problem: openings are pushed, closings must match the top of the stack, and the stack must end empty. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Check whether the bracket string '{[()]}' is balanced, and whether '((())' is balanced, using a stack. A string is balanced when every opening bracket is closed by the matching kind in the correct order.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Walk through the string left to right, pushing each opening bracket onto a stack.
  2. Step 2: On a closing bracket, pop the top of the stack: it must be the matching opening kind, or the string is unbalanced.
  3. Step 3: For {[()]}: every closing bracket matches the most recent opening bracket, and the stack ends empty, so it is balanced: True.
  4. Step 4: For ((()): a closing bracket meets the wrong opening kind (or the stack ends non-empty), so it is not balanced: False.
  5. Step 5: Check: the stack discipline is exactly the nesting rule, so the verdict follows the definition.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

{[()]} is balanced: True. ((()) is balanced: False. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same stack procedure checks any bracket string over any set of matched pairs. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds for the three bracket kinds stated; other bracket-like pairs (quotes, tags) need their own matching rules, and a string with no brackets at all is balanced by this definition. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

One pass over n characters with constant work each gives O(n) time and O(n) space for the stack. The demo checks both stated strings and prints both verdicts. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because the stack holds exactly the unclosed openings in order, so a closing bracket matches the most recent unclosed opening precisely when the nesting is correct. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'s1': '{[()]}', 's2': '((())'}
def solve(P):
    def check(s):
        pairs = {')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{'}
        stack = []
        for ch in s:
            if ch in '([{':
                stack.append(ch)
            elif ch in ')]}':
                if not stack or stack.pop() != pairs[ch]:
                    return False
        return not stack
    r1 = check(P['s1'])
    r2 = check(P['s2'])
    return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('String 1:', P['s1'])
print('Balanced 1:', R['r1'])
print('String 2:', P['s2'])
print('Balanced 2:', R['r2'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Bracket matching: curly brackets with a missing close """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'s1': '{[()]}', 's2': '((())'}
    def solve(self, P):
        def check(s):
            pairs = {')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{'}
            stack = []
            for ch in s:
                if ch in '([{':
                    stack.append(ch)
                elif ch in ')]}':
                    if not stack or stack.pop() != pairs[ch]:
                        return False
            return not stack
        r1 = check(P['s1'])
        r2 = check(P['s2'])
        return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('String 1:', P['s1'])
        print('Balanced 1:', R['r1'])
        print('String 2:', P['s2'])
        print('Balanced 2:', R['r2'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

History

Stack-based bracket matching is a standard exercise in every data-structures course and the basis of syntax checking in compilers and editors. The algorithm itself is a standard part of the computer science curriculum and of everyday programming practice: it appears in textbooks, in interview preparation, and in production code in one form or another, and the implementation here follows the textbook form, checked by running it. The record exists because an AI that writes code should be able to retrieve a known-good implementation with its trace, instead of synthesising one fresh and risking an off-by-one error or a missed edge case.

Problem

Check whether the bracket string '{[()]}' is balanced, and whether '((())' is balanced, using a stack. A string is balanced when every opening bracket is closed by the matching kind in the correct order.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Walk through the string left to right, pushing each opening bracket onto a stack. Step 2: On a closing bracket, pop the top of the stack: it must be the matching opening kind, or the string is unbalanced. Step 3: For {[()]}: every closing bracket matches the most recent opening bracket, and the stack ends empty, so it is balanced: True. Step 4: For ((()): a closing bracket meets the wrong opening kind (or the stack ends non-empty), so it is not balanced: False. Step 5: Check: the stack discipline is exactly the nesting rule, so the verdict follows the definition. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Input strings stated in the problem; stack-based matching.

Verification path

python3 -c "import json; rec=[json.loads(l) for l in open('presolved-code-algorithms-signature.jsonl', encoding='utf-8')][22]; exec(rec['demo']['code'])"

Formal equation

balanced iff stack empty at end

Formal equation, in plain words

The stack holds unclosed opening brackets in order; balanced means every opening was matched and the stack is empty at the end.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

push open, match close

Proof sketch

The solution is checked by execution. First, the demo runs the algorithm on the stated input: it runs the stack-based bracket check on both stated strings and prints the verdicts. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the edge cases named in the worked solution are also executed and their results printed. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

{[()]} is balanced: True. ((()) is balanced: False. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds for the three bracket kinds stated; other bracket-like pairs (quotes, tags) need their own matching rules, and a string with no brackets at all is balanced by this definition. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Specification

  • care: Check for a closing bracket with an empty stack before popping; that is the unbalanced case, not an error to ignore.
  • dimensions: {'s1': '{[()]}', 's2': '((())'}

  • A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the bracket matching chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'s1': '{[()]}', 's2': '((())'}
def solve(P):
    def check(s):
        pairs = {')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{'}
        stack = []
        for ch in s:
            if ch in '([{':
                stack.append(ch)
            elif ch in ')]}':
                if not stack or stack.pop() != pairs[ch]:
                    return False
        return not stack
    r1 = check(P['s1'])
    r2 = check(P['s2'])
    return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('String 1:', P['s1'])
print('Balanced 1:', R['r1'])
print('String 2:', P['s2'])
print('Balanced 2:', R['r2'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Bracket matching: curly brackets with a missing close """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'s1': '{[()]}', 's2': '((())'}
    def solve(self, P):
        def check(s):
            pairs = {')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{'}
            stack = []
            for ch in s:
                if ch in '([{':
                    stack.append(ch)
                elif ch in ')]}':
                    if not stack or stack.pop() != pairs[ch]:
                        return False
            return not stack
        r1 = check(P['s1'])
        r2 = check(P['s2'])
        return {'r1': r1, 'r2': r2}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('String 1:', P['s1'])
        print('Balanced 1:', R['r1'])
        print('String 2:', P['s2'])
        print('Balanced 2:', R['r2'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Merge two sorted lists: interleaved ranges JAH-PSCAS-000024 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a programming problem: merge two sorted lists: interleaved ranges. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Merging two sorted lists is the inner step of merge sort: compare the heads, take the smaller, and repeat until both lists are used up. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. History

Description

This record pre-solves a programming problem: merge two sorted lists: interleaved ranges. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, gives working code, traces the execution on a concrete input, and states the result. The demo that ships with the record IS the solution running: it executes the algorithm on the stated input and prints the trace and the result, so the answer is verified by execution rather than asserted. Merging two sorted lists is the inner step of merge sort: compare the heads, take the smaller, and repeat until both lists are used up. Algorithm problems of this kind are the purest presolved shape in programming, because the deliverable is code and the check is running it: the same input always gives the same output, and any bug shows up at once. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Code Algorithms Database, each covering a different algorithm, so that an AI retrieving it has the working code, the trace, and the complexity analysis ready-made.

The Problem

Merge the sorted lists [2, 5, 9, 11] and [1, 4, 7, 10, 13] into one sorted list. Give the merged list and the number of comparisons.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Point at the head of each sorted list.
  2. Step 2: Repeatedly compare the two heads and take the smaller into the output; each comparison is counted.
  3. Step 3: When one list runs out, append the rest of the other list unchanged.
  4. Step 4: The merged list is 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 9, 10, 11, 13, reached with 8 comparisons.
  5. Step 5: Check: the output is in increasing order, has 9 elements, and contains every element of both inputs exactly once.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The merged sorted list is 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 9, 10, 11, 13, reached with 8 comparisons. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated problem shape occurs: the same input type, the same required output, and inputs inside the normal range for a worked example. The same procedure merges any two sorted lists of comparable elements. It applies to learning the algorithm, checking an implementation, and AI question-answering where the code must actually run. It does not apply where the input shape differs; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if both inputs are sorted; merging unsorted lists this way does not sort them. It is stable when the left list wins ties, as here. Beyond those limits: the result holds for the stated input and for inputs of the same shape; very large inputs change the running time (see the complexity analysis) and may need an iterative rather than recursive form, more memory, or a different algorithm altogether. The record solves one problem with one set of inputs; it does not generalise itself, and it should not be quoted for inputs it does not cover. The code is a worked example, not a production library, and it carries no error handling beyond what the worked solution states.

Math Proof

Each element is taken once and each comparison takes one element, so the cost is O(n + m) time and O(n + m) space for the output. The demo counts the comparisons actually performed. The mathematical justification is the complexity and correctness argument for the algorithm: the demo counts the operations actually performed on the stated input, and that count is consistent with the claimed complexity bound. The demo evaluates the algorithm in code, so the result is machine-checked rather than hand-traced, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print.

Science Proof

The algorithm is correct because both inputs are sorted, so the smaller head is always the smallest remaining element overall; taking it next keeps the output sorted. The solution is sound because the algorithm is a deterministic procedure whose correctness follows from its own structure: each step preserves the invariant the algorithm relies on, and the trace printed by the demo shows the invariant holding at every stage until the result is produced. No step depends on an untested assumption; the code is executed, not merely described, and the test cases in the demo include the stated input and the edge cases named in the worked solution.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'a': [2, 5, 9, 11], 'b': [1, 4, 7, 10, 13]}
def solve(P):
    a = list(P['a'])
    b = list(P['b'])
    out = []
    i = j = 0
    comp = 0
    while i < len(a) and j < len(b):
        comp += 1
        if a[i] <= b[j]:
            out.append(a[i]); i += 1
        else:
            out.append(b[j]); j += 1
    out += a[i:]
    out += b[j:]
    return {'merged': out, 'merged_str': ', '.join(map(str, out)), 'comparisons': comp, 'n': len(a) + len(b)}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('List A:', P['a'])
print('List B:', P['b'])
print('Merged:', R['merged'])
print('Comparisons:', R['comparisons'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Merge two sorted lists: interleaved ranges """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'a': [2, 5, 9, 11], 'b': [1, 4, 7, 10, 13]}
    def solve(self, P):
        a = list(P['a'])
        b = list(P['b'])
        out = []
